{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 225.14 11.01 L -225.14 11.01 C -228.2 11.01 -230.68 8.53 -230.68 5.48 L -230.68 -5.48 C -230.68 -8.53 -228.2 -11.01 -225.14 -11.01 L 225.14 -11.01 C 228.2 -11.01 230.68 -8.53 230.68 -5.48 L 230.68 5.48 C 230.68 8.53 228.2 11.01 225.14 11.01 Z M -230.68 -11.01}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-165.85707pt}{-4.60358pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\\[4.30554pt]
\leadsto&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}promise}\;(\mathsf{op^{\prime}}\;y\mapsto M[V/x])\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}as}\;q\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}await}\;q\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}until}\;\langle z\rangle\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}let}\;p=M^{\prime}\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,N))\end{array}

Here, both final computations are
temporarily
blocked until an incoming interrupt
\mathsf{op^{\prime}}

is propagated to them and the variable
q
gets bound to a fulfilled promise. Until this happens,
it is not possible for the blocked continuation
N
to reduce to
N^{\prime}
in the latter final computation.

Another, distinct source of non-confluence concerns the commutativity of outgoing signals with enveloping interrupt
handlers. For instance, the following composite computation

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}promise}\;(\mathsf{op}\;x\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op^{\prime}}\,(W^{\prime},M))\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}as}\;p\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op^{\prime\prime}}\,(W^{\prime\prime},N)})

can nondeterministically reduce to either

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op^{\prime}}\,(W^{\prime},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op^{\prime\prime}}\,(W^{\prime\prime},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}let}\;p=M\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,N)}))

if we first propagate the interrupt
\mathsf{op}
inwards, or to

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op^{\prime\prime}}\,(W^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op^{\prime}}\,(W^{\prime},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}let}\;p=M\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,N)}))

if we first propagate the signal
\mathsf{op}^{\prime\prime}
outwards. As a result, in the resulting two computations,
the signals
\mathsf{op}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{op}^{\prime\prime}
get issued, and received by other processes, in a different order.

A More Efficient Operational Semantics?

Finally, it is worth emphasising that the operational semantics we present in
this paper is meant to serve as a declarative reference semantics of

\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
, and as a means to relate the behaviour of the program constructs
specific to
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
to the behaviour of conventional algebraic effects and
their handlers. As such, the semantics is clearly not as efficient as one might
desire in a real-world implementation. For instance, in the current semantics,
signals are propagated out of computations one small step at a time. Instead,
one might consider an alternative semantics in which there would be a reduction
rule to pull signals out of computations from arbitrary depths. Dually, the
propagation of interrupts into computations also happens one small step at a
time. Here one might wonder whether it could be possible to use substitution in

\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
to make that propagation more efficient, akin to how we currently
use substitution to propagate fulfilled promises to sub-computations. Yet
another approach could be to model signal and interrupt propagation using shared
channels, as noted in
Section 7
. However, as in this paper our
focus is not on the efficiency of the semantics, we leave all such explorations
for future work.

3.3.
Type-and-Effect System

We equip
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
with a type system in the tradition of type-and-effect systems for algebraic effects and
effect handlers
[
BP14
,
KLO13
]
, by extending the simple type system of FGCBV
with annotations about programs’ possible effects (such as issued signals and installed interrupt handlers)
in function and computation types.

3.3.1.
Types

We define types in
Figure 3
, separated into ground, value, and computation types.

\displaystyle\text{Ground type $A$, $B$}\mathrel{\;{:}{:}{=}\ }

\displaystyle\mathsf{b}\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}\mathsf{1}\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}\mathsf{0}\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}A\times B\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}A+B

Signal or interrupt signature:

\displaystyle\mathsf{op}:A_{\mathsf{op}}

Outgoing signal annotations:

\displaystyle o\in O

Interrupt handler annotations:

\displaystyle\iota\in I

\displaystyle\text{Value type $X$, $Y$}\mathrel{\;{:}{:}{=}\ }

\displaystyle A\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}X\times Y\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}X+Y\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}X\to Y\and(o,\iota)\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}\langle X\rangle

Computation type:

\displaystyle X\and(o,\iota)

\displaystyle\text{Typing context $\Gamma$}\mathrel{\;{:}{:}{=}\ }

\displaystyle\cdot\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}\Gamma,x\mathinner{:}X

Figure 3.
Value and Computation Types.

As noted in
Section 3.1
,
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
is parameterised over a set

\Sigma
of signal and interrupt
names
. To each such name
\mathsf{op}\in\Sigma
, we assign a
signature

\mathsf{op}:A_{\mathsf{op}}
that specifies the payload type
A_{\mathsf{op}}
of the corresponding signal or interrupt.
Crucially, in order to be able to later prove that
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
is type-safe, we must put restrictions
on these signatures, as they classify values that may cross interrupt handler or process boundaries.
In
Section 5.3
, we describe the exact reasons behind this restriction,
and propose a more flexible type system employing Fitch-style modal types
[
Clo18
]
. But for the sake of exposition, we use here
the more limited approach from our original work
[
AP21
]
, and restrict payload types to

ground types

A,B,\ldots
, which include base, unit, empty, product, and sum types, but importantly
exclude promise and function types.

Value types

X,Y,\ldots
extend ground types with function and promise types.
The
function type

X\to Y\and(o,\iota)
classifies functions that take
X
-typed arguments
to computations classified by the
computation type

Y\and(o,\iota)
, i.e., ones that return
Y
-typed
values, while possibly issuing signals specified by
o
and handling interrupts specified by
\iota
.
The
effect annotations

o
and
\iota
are drawn from sets
O
and
I
whose definitions we discuss
in
Section 3.3.2
. The
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
-specific
promise type

\langle X\rangle
classifies promises that can be fulfilled by supplying a value of type
X
.

3.3.2.
Effect Annotations

We now explain how we define the sets
O
and
I
from which we draw the
effect annotations we use for specifying functions and computations.
Traditionally, effect systems for algebraic effects simply use (flat) sets of
operation names for effect annotations
[
BP14
,
KLO13
]
.
In
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
, however, we need to be
more careful, because triggering an interrupt handler executes a computation
that can issue potentially different signals and handle different interrupts from the main
program, and we would like to capture this in types.

Signal Annotations

First, as outgoing signals do not carry any computational data, we follow
the tradition of type-and-effect systems for algebraic effects, and define

O
to be the
power set

\mathcal{P}(\Sigma)
. As such, each
o\in O
is a subset of
the signature
\Sigma
, specifying which signals a computation might issue (this is
an over-approximation of the actually issued signals).

Interrupt Handler Annotations

As observed above, for specifying installed interrupt handlers, we cannot use (flat) sets
of interrupt names as the effect annotations
\iota\in I
if we want to track the nested
(and sometimes recursive) effectful structure of interrupt handlers.

Instead, intuitively each
\iota\in I
is a

possibly infinite
nesting of partial mappings of pairs of
O
- and
I
-annotations to names in

\Sigma
—these pairs of annotations classify the possible effects of the corresponding interrupt handler code.
We use the
record notation

\iota=\{\mathsf{op}_{1}\mapsto(o_{1},\iota_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{op}_{n}\mapsto(o_{n},\iota_{n})\}

to mean that
\iota
maps
\mathsf{op}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{op}_{n}
to the annotations
(o_{1},\iota_{1}),\ldots,(o_{n},\iota_{n})
,
while any other names in
\Sigma
are unannotated, corresponding to no interrupt handlers being installed for
these other names. We write
\iota\,(\mathsf{op}_{i})=(o_{i},\iota_{i})
to mean that the annotation

\iota
maps
\mathsf{op}_{i}
to
(o_{i},\iota_{i})
.

Formally,
we define
I
as the
greatest fixed point

of a set functor
\Phi
, given by

\Phi(X)\mathrel{\overset{\text{\tiny def}}{=}}\Sigma\Rightarrow(O\times X)_{\bot}

where
\Rightarrow
is exponentiation,
\times
is Cartesian product,
and
(-)_{\bot}
is the lifting operation, which we use to represent unannotated names, and which is defined
using the disjoint union as
(-)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\{\bot\}
. Formally speaking,
I
is given
by an isomorphism
I\cong\Phi(I)
, but for presentation purposes we leave it
implicit and work as if we had a strict equality
I=\Phi(I)
.

Subtyping and Recursive Effect Annotations

Both
O
and
I
come equipped with natural
partial orders
: for
O
,
\sqsubseteq_{O}
is given simply by
subset inclusion; and for
I
, the pointwise order
\sqsubseteq_{I}
is characterised as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l c l}\iota\sqsubseteq_{I}\iota^{\prime}&\text{iff}&\forall\,(\mathsf{op}\in\Sigma)\,(o^{\prime\prime}\in O)\,(\iota^{\prime\prime}\in I).\,\iota\,(\mathsf{op})=({o^{\prime\prime}},{\iota^{\prime\prime}})\implies\\[2.15277pt]
&&\exists\,(o^{\prime\prime\prime}\in O)\,(\iota^{\prime\prime\prime}\in I).\,\iota^{\prime}\,(\mathsf{op})=({o^{\prime\prime\prime}},{\iota^{\prime\prime\prime}})\wedge o^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq_{O}o^{\prime\prime\prime}\wedge\iota^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq_{I}\iota^{\prime\prime\prime}\end{array}

We also use the
product order

\sqsubseteq_{O\times I}
, defined as

(o,\iota)\sqsubseteq_{O\times I}(o^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})\mathrel{\overset{\text{\tiny def}}{=}}o\sqsubseteq_{O}o^{\prime}\wedge\iota\sqsubseteq_{I}\iota^{\prime}
.
In particular, we use
\sqsubseteq_{O\times I}
to define the subtyping
relation for
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
’s computation types.

Furthermore, both
O
and
I
carry a
join-semilattice
structure, where

o\sqcup o^{\prime}\in O
is given simply by the union of sets
o\cup o^{\prime}
, while

\iota\sqcup\iota^{\prime}\in I
is given pointwise as follows:

(\iota\sqcup\iota^{\prime})(\mathsf{op})~\mathrel{\overset{\text{\tiny def}}{=}}~\begin{cases}(o^{\prime\prime}\sqcup o^{\prime\prime\prime},\iota^{\prime\prime}\sqcup\iota^{\prime\prime\prime})&\mbox{if }\iota\,(\mathsf{op})=(o^{\prime\prime},\iota^{\prime\prime})\wedge\iota^{\prime}\,(\mathsf{op})=(o^{\prime\prime\prime},\iota^{\prime\prime\prime})\\
(o^{\prime\prime},\iota^{\prime\prime})&\mbox{if }\iota\,(\mathsf{op})=(o^{\prime\prime},\iota^{\prime\prime})\wedge\iota^{\prime}\,(\mathsf{op})=\bot\\
(o^{\prime\prime\prime},\iota^{\prime\prime\prime})&\mbox{if }\iota\,(\mathsf{op})=\bot\wedge\iota^{\prime}\,(\mathsf{op})=(o^{\prime\prime\prime},\iota^{\prime\prime\prime})\\
\bot&\mbox{if }\iota\,(\mathsf{op})=\bot\wedge\iota^{\prime}\,(\mathsf{op})=\bot\\
\end{cases}

Importantly, the partial orders
(O,\sqsubseteq_{O})
and
(I,\sqsubseteq_{I})
are both
\omega
-complete
and
pointed
, i.e.,
they form
pointed
\omega
-cpos
, meaning that they have least upper bounds of all increasing
\omega
-chains, and
least elements (given by the empty set
\emptyset
and the constant
\bot
-valued mapping, respectively).
As a consequence, and as is well-known,
least fixed points
of continuous (endo)maps on them are then guaranteed
to exist
[
AC98
,
GHK+03
]
.
For
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
, we are particularly interested in the least fixed points of continuous maps
f:I\to I
,
so as to specify and typecheck code examples involving reinstallable interrupt handlers, as we illustrate in

Section 5.1
.

We also note that if we were only interested in the type safety of
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
, and not
in typechecking reinstallable interrupt handler examples, then we would not need
(I,\sqsubseteq_{I})
to be
\omega
-complete
,
and could have instead chosen
I
to be the

least fixed point
of the set functor
\Phi
defined earlier, which is what we do for simplicity in our
Agda

formalisation. In this case, each interrupt handler annotation
\iota\in I
would be a
finite
nesting of partial mappings.

Finally, we envisage that any future full-fledged high-level language based on
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
would
allow users to define their (recursive) effect annotations in a small domain-specific language, providing
a syntactic counterpart to the domain-theoretic development we use in this paper.

Interrupt Actions

We mimic the act of triggering an interrupt handler for some interrupt
\mathsf{op}
on an effect annotation
(o,\iota)
through an

action
defined as follows:

{\mathsf{op}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}}\,(o,\iota)~\mathrel{\overset{\text{\tiny def}}{=}}~\begin{cases}\left(o\sqcup o^{\prime},\iota[\mathsf{op}\mapsto\bot]\sqcup\iota^{\prime}\right)&\mbox{if }\iota\,(\mathsf{op})=(o^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})\\
(o,\iota)&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}

If
(o,\iota)
lists any interrupt handlers installed for
\mathsf{op}
, then
\iota\,(\mathsf{op})=(o^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})
,
where
(o^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})
specifies the effects of said handler code. Now, when the inward propagating
interrupt reaches those interrupt handlers, it triggers the execution of the corresponding handler code,
and thus the entire interrupted computation can also issue signals in
o^{\prime}
and handle interrupts in
\iota^{\prime}
.

The notation
\iota[\mathsf{op}\mapsto\bot]
sets
\iota
to
\bot
at
\mathsf{op}
,
and leaves it unchanged elsewhere.
Mapping
\mathsf{op}
to
\bot
in the definition of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}
captures that the interrupt
\mathsf{op}
triggers all the corresponding interrupt handlers that are installed in the computation that it is propagated to.

3.3.3.
Typing Rules

We characterise
well-typed values
using the judgement
\Gamma\vdash V:X

and
well-typed computations
using the judgement
\Gamma\vdash M:X\and(o,\iota)
.
In both judgements,
\Gamma
is a
typing context
.
The rules defining these judgements are respectively given in
Figure 4
and

5
.

\frac{}{\Gamma,x\mathinner{:}X,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash x:X}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyVal-Var}}\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash():\mathsf{1}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyVal-Unit}}\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{\Gamma\vdash V:X\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash W:Y}{\Gamma\vdash(V,W):X\times Y}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyVal-Pair}}\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{\Gamma\vdash V:X}{\Gamma\vdash\langle V\rangle:\langle X\rangle}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyVal-Promise}}\\
\frac{\Gamma\vdash V:X}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}inl}_{Y}\,V:X+Y}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyVal-Inl}}\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{\Gamma\vdash W:Y}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}inr}_{X}\,W:X+Y}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyVal-Inr}}\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{\Gamma,x\mathinner{:}X\vdash M:Y\and(o,\iota)}{\Gamma\vdash{\mathop{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}fun}}}\;(x:X)\mapsto M:X\to Y\and(o,\iota)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyVal-Fun}}

Figure 4.
Value Typing Rules.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash V:X}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}return}\,V:X\and(o,\iota)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyComp-Return}}

\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:X\and(o,\iota)\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma,x\mathinner{:}X\vdash N:Y\and(o,\iota)}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}let}\;x=M\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;N:Y\and(o,\iota)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyComp-Let}}

\frac{\Gamma\vdash V:X\to Y\and(o,\iota)\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma\vdash W:X}{\Gamma\vdash V\,W:Y\and(o,\iota)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyComp-Apply}}

\frac{\Gamma\vdash V:X\times Y\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma,x\mathinner{:}X,y\mathinner{:}Y\vdash M:Z\and(o,\iota)}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}match}\;V\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}with}\;\{(x,y)\mapsto M\}:Z\and(o,\iota)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyComp-MatchPair}}

\frac{\Gamma\vdash V:\mathsf{0}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}match}\;V\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}with}\;\{\}_{Z\and(o,\iota)}:Z\and(o,\iota)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyComp-MatchEmpty}}

\frac{\Gamma\vdash V:X+Y\hskip 8.04996pt\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma,x\mathinner{:}X\vdash M:Z\and(o,\iota)\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma,y\mathinner{:}Y\vdash N:Z\and(o,\iota)\hskip 8.04996pt}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}match}\;V\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}with}\;\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}inl}\,x\mapsto M,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}inr}\,y\mapsto N\}:Z\and(o,\iota)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyComp-MatchSum}}

\frac{\mathsf{op}\in o\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma\vdash V:A_{\mathsf{op}}\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma\vdash M:X\and(o,\iota)}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,M):X\and(o,\iota)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyComp-Signal}}

\frac{\Gamma\vdash V:A_{\mathsf{op}}\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma\vdash M:X\and(o,\iota)}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,M):X\and{\mathsf{op}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}}\,(o,\iota)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyComp-Interrupt}}

\frac{({o^{\prime}},{\iota^{\prime}})=\iota\,(\mathsf{op})\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma,x\mathinner{:}A_{\mathsf{op}}\vdash M:\langle X\rangle\and(o^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma,p\mathinner{:}\langle X\rangle\vdash N:Y\and(o,\iota)}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}promise}\;(\mathsf{op}\;x\mapsto M)\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}as}\;p\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;N:Y\and(o,\iota)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyComp-Promise}}

\frac{\Gamma\vdash V:\langle X\rangle\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma,x\mathinner{:}X\vdash M:Y\and(o,\iota)}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}await}\;V\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}until}\;\langle x\rangle\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;M:Y\and(o,\iota)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyComp-Await}}

\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:X\and(o,\iota)\hskip 8.04996pt(o,\iota)\sqsubseteq_{O\times I}(o^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})}{\Gamma\vdash M:X\and(o^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyComp-Subsume}}

Figure 5.
Computation Typing Rules.

Values

The rules for values are mostly standard.
The only
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
-specific rule is
TyVal-Promise
, which states that in order to fulfil
a
promise
of type
\langle X\rangle
, one has to supply a value of type
X
. In the rule
TyVal-Var
, we emphasise the position of the variable in the context, as it will become important once we extend the calculus with modal types in
Section 5.3
.

Computations

Analogously to values, the typing rules are standard for computation terms that
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
inherits from FGCBV,
with the
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
-rules additionally tracking effect information.

The first
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
-specific typing rule
TyComp-Signal
states that in order
to issue a signal
\mathsf{op}
in a computation that has type
X\and(o,\iota)
, we must have
\mathsf{op}\in o
and the type of
the payload value has to match
\mathsf{op}
’s signature
\mathsf{op}:A_{\mathsf{op}}
.

The rule
TyComp-Interrupt
is used to type incoming interrupts.
In particular, when the outside world propagates an interrupt
\mathsf{op}
to a computation

M
of type
X\and(o,\iota)
, the resulting
computation
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,M)
gets assigned the type
X\and{\mathsf{op}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}}\,(o,\iota)
,
where the action
{\mathsf{op}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}}\,(o,\iota)
of the interrupt
\mathsf{op}
on the annotation
(o,\iota)
is given as
discussed in
Section 3.3.2
.

The rule
TyComp-Promise
states that
the interrupt handler code
M
has to return a fulfilled promise of type
\langle X\rangle
, for some type
X
,
while possibly issuing signals
o^{\prime}
and handling interrupts
\iota^{\prime}
, both of which are
determined by the effect annotation
\iota
of the entire computation, as

(o^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})=\iota\,(\mathsf{op})
. The variable
p
bound in the continuation, which sub-computations can block on
to await
\mathsf{op}
to arrive and be handled, also gets assigned
the promise type
\langle X\rangle
.

It is worth noting that we could have had
M
simply
return values of type
X
, but at the cost of not being able to implement some of the more interesting examples,
such as the guarded interrupt handlers defined in
Section 6.1
.
At the same time, for
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
’s type safety, it is
crucial that
p
would have remained assigned the distinguished promise type
\langle X\rangle
.

The rule
TyComp-Await
simply states that after awaiting a promise of type
\langle X\rangle
,
the continuation
M
can refer to the promised value using the variable
x
of type
X
.

Finally, the rule
TyComp-Subsume
allows
subtyping
, required to prove type preservation for rules where an interrupt encounters an interrupt handler.
To simplify the presentation, we consider a limited form of subtyping, in which we
shallowly relate only effect annotations.

3.4.
Type Safety

The sequential part of
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
satisfies the expected type safety properties
ensuring that “well-typed programs do not go wrong”. We split these safety properties into the usual

progress
and
preservation
theorems
[
WF94
]
.
We omit their proofs
[
AP21
]
from this summary, and revisit them
in
Section 5.5
for the extended version of
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
,
as the proofs for the extended calculus also apply to the version summarised in this section.

The progress result states that well-typed (and sufficiently) closed computations can either make another step of
reduction, or they are already in a well-defined result form (and thus have correctly stopped reducing).
As such, we first need to define when we consider
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
-computations
to be in
result form
(commonly also called a normal form). We do so using the
judgements
\mathsf{CompRes}\langle\Psi\,|\,M\rangle
,
which states that
M
has reached its final form as an isolated computation term,
and
\mathsf{RunRes}\langle\Psi\,|\,M\rangle
, which states that
M
has reached the final form of a
computation running inside a process with all its signals already having been propagated to
other parallel processes (described in more detail in
Section 4.4
):

\frac{\mathsf{CompRes}\langle\Psi\,|\,M\rangle}{\mathsf{CompRes}\langle\Psi\,|\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,M)\rangle}\quad\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}}}\frac{\mathsf{RunRes}\langle\Psi\,|\,M\rangle}{\mathsf{CompRes}\langle\Psi\,|\,M\rangle}\quad\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}}}\frac{}{\mathsf{RunRes}\langle\Psi\,|\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}return}\,V\rangle}\quad\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}}}\frac{\mathsf{RunRes}\langle\Psi\cup\{p\}\,|\,N\rangle}{\mathsf{RunRes}\langle\Psi\,|\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}promise}\;(\mathsf{op}\;x\mapsto M)\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}as}\;p\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;N\rangle}\quad\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}}}\frac{p\in\Psi}{\mathsf{RunRes}\langle\Psi\,|\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}await}\;p\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}until}\;\langle x\rangle\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;M\rangle}\quad\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}}}

In these judgements,
\Psi
is a set
of (promise-typed) variables
p
that have been bound by interrupt handlers enveloping the given computation.
Intuitively, these judgements express that a computation
M
is in a (top-level)
result form
\mathsf{CompRes}\langle\Psi\,|\,M\rangle
when, considered as a tree, it has a shape in which
all

signals are towards the root, interrupt handlers are in the intermediate nodes, and
the leaves contain return values and computations that are temporarily blocked
while awaiting one of the promise-typed variables
p
in
\Psi
to be fulfilled.

The new reduction rules that propagate the awaiting construct out of sequencing and interrupts into the awaiting construct ensure the explicit form of all blocking computations and considerably simplify the definition of
\mathsf{RunRes}\langle\Psi\,|\,M\rangle
compared to the previous version of our work
[
AP21
]
.
The finality of these result forms is captured by the next lemma.

Lemma 1
.

Given
\Psi
and
M
, such that
\mathsf{CompRes}\langle\Psi\,|\,M\rangle
, then there is no
N
with
M\leadsto N
.

Using the result forms, the progress theorem for the sequential part of
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
is as follows:

Theorem 2

(Progress for computations)
.

Given a well-typed computation

p_{1}\mathinner{:}\langle X_{1}\rangle,\ldots,p_{n}\mathinner{:}\langle X_{n}\rangle\vdash M:Y\and(o,\iota)

then either

(1)

there exists a computation
N
, such that
M\leadsto N
, or

(2)

the computation
M
is in a result form, i.e., we have
\mathsf{CompRes}\langle\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}\,|\,M\rangle
.

In particular, with the empty context, we get the usual progress statement, which states that

\vdash M:X\and(o,\iota)
implies that either
M\leadsto N
for some
N

or that
\mathsf{CompRes}\langle\emptyset\,|\,M\rangle
holds. This implies that any promise variable
which we are awaiting to be fulfilled must correspond to one of the installed interrupt handlers.
Additionally, the type system ensures that all outgoing signals are listed in
o
and all
installed interrupt handlers are specified in
\iota
.

The type preservation result is standard and says that reduction preserves well-typedness.

Theorem 3

(Preservation for computations)
.

Given a computation
\Gamma\vdash M:X\and(o,\iota)
, such that
M

can reduce as
M\leadsto N
, then we have
\Gamma\vdash N:X\and(o,\iota)
.

4.
A Calculus for Asynchronous Effects: Parallel Processes

We now describe the parallel part of
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
. Similarly to the sequential part, we
present the corresponding syntax, small-step semantics,
type-and-effect system, and type safety results.

4.1.
Parallel Processes

To keep the presentation focussed on the asynchronous use of algebraic effects, we
consider a very simple model of parallelism: a process is either an
individual computation

or the
parallel composition
of two processes. To facilitate interactions, processes also
contain outward propagating
signals
and inward propagating
interrupts
.

In detail, the syntax of
parallel processes
is given by the following grammar:

P,Q\mathrel{\;{:}{:}{=}\ }\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}run}\;M\,\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}\!P\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}Q\,\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}\!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,P)\,\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}\!\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,P)

Note that processes do not include interrupt handlers—these are local to computations.

Here the number and hierarchy of processes running in parallel is fixed—a limitation that we address
in
Section 5.4
by introducing a means to dynamically create new processes.

4.2.
Small-Step Operational Semantics

We equip the parallel part of
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
with a small-step operational semantics that
naturally extends the semantics of
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
’s sequential part from
Section 3.2
.
The semantics is defined using a reduction relation
P\leadsto Q
, as given in
Figure 6
.

Individual computations

\displaystyle\frac{M\leadsto N}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}run}\;M\leadsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}run}\;N}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}}}

Signal hoisting

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}run}\;(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,M))

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}run}\;M)

Broadcasting

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,P)\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}Q

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,P\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,Q))

\displaystyle P\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,Q)

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,P)\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}Q)

Interrupt propagation

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}run}\;M)

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}run}\;(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,M))

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,P\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}Q)

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,P)\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,Q)

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op^{\prime}}\,(W,P))

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op^{\prime}}\,(W,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,P))

Evaluation context rule

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{P\leadsto Q}{\mathcal{F}[P]\leadsto\mathcal{F}[Q]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}}}

where

\displaystyle\text{$\mathcal{F}$}\mathrel{\;{:}{:}{=}\ }

\displaystyle[~]\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}\mathcal{F}\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}Q\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}\!P\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}\mathcal{F}\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,\mathcal{F})\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,\mathcal{F})

Figure 6.
Small-Step Operational Semantics of Processes.

Individual Computations

This rule states that, as processes, individual computations evolve according to the small-step
operational semantics
M\leadsto N
we defined in
Section 3.2
.

Signal Hoisting

This rule propagates signals out of individual computations.
Note that we only hoist those signals that have propagated to the outer boundary
of a computation.

Broadcasting

These rules turn outward moving signals in one process into inward moving interrupts
for the process parallel to it, while continuing to propagate the signals outwards to any
further parallel processes. The latter ensures that the semantics is compositional.

Interrupt Propagation

These three rules simply propagate interrupts inwards into individual computations,
into all branches of parallel compositions, and past any issued signals.

Evaluation Contexts

Analogously to the semantics of computations, the semantics of processes presented here also
includes an evaluation context rule, which allows reductions under
evaluation contexts

\mathcal{F}
. Observe that compared to the evaluation contexts for computations, those for processes
are more standard, in the sense that they do not bind variables.

4.3.
Type-and-Effect System

Analogously to its sequential part, we also equip
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
’s parallel part with a type-and-effect system.

Types

The
process types
are designed to match their parallel structure, and
are given by

\text{$C$, $D$}\mathrel{\;{:}{:}{=}\ }X\mathbin{!!}(o,\iota)\mathrel{\,\;\big|\ \ \!}C\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}D

Namely,
X\mathbin{!!}(o,\iota)
is a type of an individual computation of type
X\and(o,\iota)
, and
C\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}D

is the type of the parallel composition of two processes that respectively have types
C
and
D
.

Typing Judgements

Well-typed processes
are characterised using the judgement

\Gamma\vdash P:C
. The typing rules are given in
Figure 7
.
While our processes are not currently higher-order, we allow
non-empty contexts
\Gamma
to model using libraries and top-level function definitions.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:X\and(o,\iota)}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}run}\;M:X\mathbin{!!}(o,\iota)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyProc-Run}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash P:C\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash Q:D}{\Gamma\vdash P\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}Q:C\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}D}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyProc-Par}}\\
\frac{\mathsf{op}\in\mathsf{signals\text{-}of}{(C)}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash V:A_{\mathsf{op}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash P:C}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,P):C}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyProc-Signal}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash V:A_{\mathsf{op}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash P:C}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,P):{\mathsf{op}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}}\,C}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyProc-Interrupt}}

Figure 7.
Process Typing Rules.

The rules
TyProc-Run
and
TyProc-Par
capture the earlier
intuition about the types of processes matching their parallel structure. The rules

TyProc-Signal
and
TyProc-Interrupt
are similar to the corresponding
computation typing rules from
Figure 5
.

The
signal annotations
of a process type used in
TyProc-Signal
are calculated as

\mathsf{signals\text{-}of}(X\mathbin{!!}(o,\iota))~\mathrel{\overset{\text{\tiny def}}{=}}~o\qquad\qquad\mathsf{signals\text{-}of}(C\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}D)~\mathrel{\overset{\text{\tiny def}}{=}}~\mathsf{signals\text{-}of}(C)\sqcup\mathsf{signals\text{-}of}(D)

and the
action of interrupts
on process types extends the action on effect annotations as

{\mathsf{op}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}}\,(X\mathbin{!!}(o,\iota))~\mathrel{\overset{\text{\tiny def}}{=}}~X\mathbin{!!}({\mathsf{op}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}}\,(o,\iota))\qquad\qquad{\mathsf{op}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}}\,(C\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}D)~\mathrel{\overset{\text{\tiny def}}{=}}~({\mathsf{op}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}}\,C)\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}({\mathsf{op}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}}\,D)

by propagating the interrupt towards the types of individual computations.

It is worth noting that
Figure 7
does not include an analogue
of the computation subtyping rule
TyComp-Subsume
. This choice is
deliberate because as we shall see below,
process types reduce

in conjunction with the processes they are assigned to, and the outcome
of process type reduction is generally neither a sub- nor supertype of the original type.

4.4.
Type Safety

We conclude summarising the meta-theory of
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
by stating the type safety
of its parallel part. Analogously to
Section 3.4
,
we once again split type safety into separate
progress

and
preservation
results, and relegate their proofs to
Section 5.5
.

We characterise the
result forms
of processes
by defining two judgements,
\mathsf{ProcRes}\langle P\rangle
and
\mathsf{ParRes}\langle P\rangle
,
and by using the judgement
\mathsf{RunRes}\langle\Psi\,|\,M\rangle
from

Section 3.4
, as follows:

\frac{\mathsf{ProcRes}\langle P\rangle}{\mathsf{ProcRes}\langle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,P)\rangle}\quad\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}}}\qquad\frac{\mathsf{ParRes}\langle P\rangle}{\mathsf{ProcRes}\langle P\rangle}\quad\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}}}\qquad\frac{\mathsf{RunRes}\langle\emptyset\,|\,M\rangle}{\mathsf{ParRes}\langle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}run}\;M\rangle}\quad\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}}}\qquad\frac{\mathsf{ParRes}\langle P\rangle\quad\mathsf{ParRes}\langle Q\rangle}{\mathsf{ParRes}\langle P\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}Q\rangle}\quad\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}}}

These judgements express that a process
P
is in a (top-level)
result form
\mathsf{ProcRes}\langle P\rangle
when, considered as a tree, it has a shape in which

all
signals are towards the root, parallel compositions are in
the intermediate nodes, and individual computation results are at the leaves.
Importantly, the computation results
\mathsf{RunRes}\langle\emptyset\,|\,M\rangle
we use in this definition
are those from which all signals have been propagated out of
(as discussed in
Section 3.4
).

Again, these result forms are operationally final, as captured by the next lemma.

Lemma 4
.

Given a process
P
, such that
\mathsf{ProcRes}\langle P\rangle
, then there is no
Q
such that
P\leadsto Q
.

We are now ready to state the progress theorem for the parallel part of
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
,
which applies to closed processes and takes the expected form:

Theorem 5

(Progress for processes)
.

Given a well-typed process
\vdash P:C
, then either

(1)

there exists a process
Q
, such that
P\leadsto Q
, or

(2)

the process
P
is already in a (top-level) result form, i.e., we have
\mathsf{ProcRes}\langle P\rangle
.

The preservation theorem for processes that we state below is somewhat non-standard since term reductions
also evolve effect annotations. In particular, the broadcast rule

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,P)\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}Q\leadsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\uparrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,P\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,Q))

and its symmetric counterpart from
Figure 6
introduce new
inward propagating interrupts in their right-hand sides that originally do not exist in their left-hand sides. As a result,
compared to the types one assigns to the left-hand sides of these reduction rules, the types assigned to
their right-hand sides will need to feature corresponding type-level actions of these interrupts.
We formalise this idea using a
process type reduction
relation
C\rightsquigarrow D
:

\frac{}{X\mathbin{!!}(o,\iota)\rightsquigarrow X\mathbin{!!}(o,\iota)}\quad\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}}}\hskip 2.0pt\frac{}{X\mathbin{!!}({\mathsf{ops}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow\downarrow}}\,(o,\iota))\rightsquigarrow X\mathbin{!!}({\mathsf{ops}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow\downarrow}}\,({\mathsf{op}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}}\,(o,\iota)))}\quad\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}}}\hskip 2.0pt\frac{C\rightsquigarrow C^{\prime}\quad D\rightsquigarrow D^{\prime}}{C\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}D\rightsquigarrow C^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}||}}D^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}}}

where we write
{\mathsf{ops}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow\downarrow}}\,(o,\iota)
for a recursively defined
action of a list of interrupts
on
(o,\iota)
:

{\mathsf{[]}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow\downarrow}}\,(o,\iota)~\mathrel{\overset{\text{\tiny def}}{=}}~(o,\iota)\qquad{\mathsf{(\mathsf{op}::\mathsf{ops})}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow\downarrow}}\,(o,\iota)~\mathrel{\overset{\text{\tiny def}}{=}}~{\mathsf{op}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}}\,({\mathsf{ops}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow\downarrow}}\,(o,\iota))

Intuitively,
C\rightsquigarrow D
describes how process types reduce by being acted upon by
freshly arriving interrupts.
It is important that we introduce interrupts under an arbitrary enveloping sequence of interrupt actions,
and not simply as
X\mathbin{!!}{(o,\iota)}\rightsquigarrow X\mathbin{!!}({\mathsf{op}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}}\,(o,\iota))
,
because we want to ensure that these actions preserve type reductions (see
Lemma
12
(3)
),
which in turn ensures type preservation of reductions under arbitrary evaluation contexts
\mathcal{F}
.

Using the process type reduction relation, we state the preservation theorem for the parallel part of

\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
as follows:

Theorem 6

(Preservation for processes)
.

Given a well-typed process
\Gamma\vdash P:C
, such that
P
can reduce as

P\leadsto Q
, then there exists a process type
D
, such
that the process type
C
can reduce as
C\rightsquigarrow D
,
and we can type the resulting process as
\Gamma\vdash Q:D
.

5.
Higher-Order Extensions

While
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
, as introduced in our original work
[
AP21
]
and summarised in the previous
two sections, can be used to naturally capture a wide range of asynchronous examples,
it also has many notable
limitations
: interrupt handlers disappear immediately after being triggered by a
matching interrupt, payloads of signals and interrupts have to be ground values, and it is not possible to
dynamically create new parallel processes. In this section we introduce and discuss a number of

higher-order extensions
of
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
that resolve these limitations. Below we discuss each of these
extensions individually, with the full extended calculus given in
Appendix A
.
We highlight the parts of
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
that change in this section’s extensions with a grey background.

5.1.
Reinstallable Interrupt Handlers

We recall from the reduction rules in
Figure 2
that
once an interrupt reaches a matching interrupt handler, the handling computation is executed
and the handler is removed. However, the example from
Section 2.7

shows that we often want to keep the handler around, e.g., to handle further interrupts
of the same kind. One option to achieve this is through general recursion
[
AP21
]
.
Unfortunately, this results in programmers defining many auxiliary functions, obfuscating the resulting code.
Furthermore, the heavy reliance on general recursion makes it difficult to justify leaving it out of the core
calculus, despite it being an orthogonal concern to many programming abstractions and, in particular, to how
we model asynchrony in
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
based on algebraic effects—this is of course not to say that a
higher-level language based on
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
could not include general recursion.

Instead, in this paper we propose extending
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
’s interrupt handlers with the ability to

reinstall
themselves, by extending the syntax for interrupt handlers given in
Section 3

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}promise}\;(\mathsf{op}\;x\mapsto M)\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}as}\;p\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;N

with an additional variable
r
bound to a function through which
M
can reinstall the handler:

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}promise}\;(\mathsf{op}\;x\,\hbox to11.5pt{\vbox to6.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.74826pt\lower-3.00635pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.42 4.16 L -2.42 4.16 C -5.48 4.16 -7.95 1.68 -7.95 -1.37 L -7.95 1.37 C -7.95 -1.68 -5.48 -4.16 -2.42 -4.16 L 2.42 -4.16 C 5.48 -4.16 7.95 -1.68 7.95 1.37 L 7.95 -1.37 C 7.95 1.68 5.48 4.16 2.42 4.16 Z M -7.95 -4.16}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.89468pt}{-2.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\mapsto M)\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}as}\;p\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;N

In contrast to the continuation/resumption variables of ordinary effect handlers, here the variable
r
does not refer
to the continuation of the interrupt at the time triggering, but instead to the act of reinstalling the given interrupt handler.
Concretely, the triggering of reinstallable interrupt handlers is captured operationally with the following
reduction rule:

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}promise}\;(\mathsf{op}\;x\,r\mapsto M)\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}as}\;p\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;N)\\
\leadsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}let}\;p=M[V/x,\hbox to198.63pt{\vbox to11.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 99.31624pt\lower-5.85358pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 131.89 8.1 L -131.89 8.1 C -134.95 8.1 -137.42 5.62 -137.42 2.56 L -137.42 -2.56 C -137.42 -5.62 -134.95 -8.1 -131.89 -8.1 L 131.89 -8.1 C 134.95 -8.1 137.42 -5.62 137.42 -2.56 L 137.42 2.56 C 137.42 5.62 134.95 8.1 131.89 8.1 Z M -137.42 -8.1}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-98.46266pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}]\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,N)

All other reduction rules remain the same, except that interrupt handlers are extended with the additional variables
r
.
Server-like processes can then be written more concisely, as

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}promise}\;(\mathsf{request}\;x\,r\mapsto\text{handle the
$\mathsf{request}$};\text{issue a $\mathsf{response}$ signal};\hbox to15.94pt{\vbox to11.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.97047pt\lower-5.85358pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.49 8.1 L -5.49 8.1 C -8.55 8.1 -11.03 5.62 -11.03 2.56 L -11.03 -2.56 C -11.03 -5.62 -8.55 -8.1 -5.49 -8.1 L 5.49 -8.1 C 8.55 -8.1 11.03 -5.62 11.03 -2.56 L 11.03 2.56 C 11.03 5.62 8.55 8.1 5.49 8.1 Z M -11.03 -8.1}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-7.1169pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}as}\;p\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}return}\,()

In light of the similarity between interrupt propagation and deep effect handling, as discussed in

Section 3.2
, this reinstalling behaviour can be
understood as an effect handler re-calling (in its corresponding operation case) the algebraic
operation that it is handling, such as, an exception handler handling an exception and then re-raising it at the end for other, external exception handlers.

The typing rule for reinstallable interrupt handlers is also quite interesting:

\frac{({o^{\prime}},{\iota^{\prime}})\mathrel{\hbox to26.62pt{\vbox to10.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 13.31047pt\lower-5.2001pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 12.88 7.2 L -12.88 7.2 C -15.94 7.2 -18.42 4.72 -18.42 1.66 L -18.42 -1.66 C -18.42 -4.72 -15.94 -7.2 -12.88 -7.2 L 12.88 -7.2 C 15.94 -7.2 18.42 -4.72 18.42 -1.66 L 18.42 1.66 C 18.42 4.72 15.94 7.2 12.88 7.2 Z M -18.42 -7.2}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-12.4569pt}{-2.01318pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\iota\,(\mathsf{op})\quad\Gamma,x\mathinner{:}A_{\mathsf{op}},\hbox to100.54pt{\vbox to13.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 50.26794pt\lower-6.85358pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 64.02 9.48 L -64.02 9.48 C -67.08 9.48 -69.56 7.01 -69.56 3.95 L -69.56 -3.95 C -69.56 -7.01 -67.08 -9.48 -64.02 -9.48 L 64.02 -9.48 C 67.08 -9.48 69.56 -7.01 69.56 -3.95 L 69.56 3.95 C 69.56 7.01 67.08 9.48 64.02 9.48 Z M -69.56 -9.48}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-49.41437pt}{-3.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\vdash M:\langle X\rangle\and(o^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})\quad\Gamma,p\mathinner{:}\langle X\rangle\vdash N:Y\and(o,\iota)}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}promise}\;(\mathsf{op}\;x\,r\mapsto M)\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}as}\;p\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;N:Y\and(o,\iota)}\quad\textsc{Lab={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}TyComp-RePromise}}

First, observe that the context in which we type the interrupt handler code
M
is now extended
with the variable
r
, which denotes a function triggered by application to the unit value

()\mathinner{:}\mathsf{1}
. The function does not emit any signals nor install any handlers apart from the one
in question for
\mathsf{op}
,
therefore its effect annotation is
\big(\emptyset,\{\mathsf{op}\mapsto({o^{\prime}},{\iota^{\prime}})\}\big)
, as expected.

Second, we have relaxed the requirement
({o^{\prime}},{\iota^{\prime}})=\iota\,(\mathsf{op})
. We now only require the effect
annotation
({o^{\prime}},{\iota^{\prime}})
of the handler code
M
to be contained in what the effect annotation
(o,\iota)
of the continuation

N
, and thus of the entire composite computation
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}promise}\;(\mathsf{op}\;x\,r\mapsto M)\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}as}\;p\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;N
, assigns to
\mathsf{op}
, i.e.,
({o^{\prime}},{\iota^{\prime}})\mathrel{\sqsubseteq_{O\times I}}\iota\,(\mathsf{op})
. The reason lies in the
proof of type preservation (see
10
) when propagating unhandled
interrupts past handlers:

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op^{\prime}}\,(V,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}promise}\;(\mathsf{op}\;x\,r\mapsto M)\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}as}\;p\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;N)\leadsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}promise}\;(\mathsf{op}\;x\,r\mapsto M)\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}as}\;p\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op^{\prime}}\,(V,N)

On the left-hand side of this reduction rule, the effect annotation of the continuation of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}promise}
is
(o,\iota)
,
while on the right-hand side it is
{\mathsf{op^{\prime}}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}}\,({o},{\iota})
. This mismatch did not pose a problem
earlier
[
AP21
]
as subtyping allowed us to increase the effect
annotation of
M
to
\pi_{2}\,({\mathsf{op^{\prime}}}\,{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}}\,({o},{\iota}))(\mathsf{op})
. Now on the other hand, as
M
’s effect annotation
is also present in the type of
r
, it appears both co- and contravariantly, and is thus not safe to increase.
However, the tight coupling of the effect annotations is not really essential, as for safety
it is enough that the annotation of the continuation simply encompasses any effects that
M
may trigger.

As noted in
Section 3.3.2
, assigning types to reinstallable handlers
requires us to consider least fixed points of continuous maps on the
\omega
-cpo
(I,\sqsubseteq_{I})
of interrupt handler annotations.
As an example, we recall the following fragment of the server code from
Section 2.7.2
:

⬇

…

promise

(
batchSizeReq

()

r

|->

send

batchSizeResp

batchSize
;

r

()

)

…

Here, the interrupt handler for
\mathsf{batchSizeReq}
reinstalls itself immediately
after issuing a
\mathsf{batchSizeResp}
signal.
Due to its recursive definition,
it should not be surprising that this handler’s effect annotation is given recursively, in
particular, if we want to give it a more precise type-level specification than one which simply states that any effect is possible.

To that end, we assign this interrupt handler the effect annotation
(\emptyset,\iota_{\text{b}})
, where

\iota_{\text{b}}=\big\{~\mathsf{batchSizeReq}\mapsto(\{\mathsf{batchSizeResp}\},\{~\mathsf{batchSizeReq}\mapsto(\{\mathsf{batchSizeResp}\},~\ldots~)~\})~\big\}

More precisely,
\iota_{\text{b}}
is the least fixed point of the following continuous map on
I
:

\iota\mapsto\{~\mathsf{batchSizeReq}\mapsto(\{\mathsf{batchSizeResp}\},\iota)~\}:I\to I

This least fixed point exists because
I
is an
\omega
-cpo and the map is continuous
(see
Section 3.3.2
).

Returning to the example above, the effect annotation
(\emptyset,\iota_{\text{b}})

specifies that the interrupt handler does not issue any signals at the top level,
and that every
\mathsf{batchSizeReq}

interrupt causes a
\mathsf{batchSizeResp}
signal to be issued and the interrupt handler
to be reinstalled.

The examples of reinstallable interrupt handlers that we discuss in
Section 6

have their effect annotations assigned analogously, also as least fixed points of
continuous maps on
I
.

5.2.
Stateful Reinstallable Interrupt Handlers

When working with reinstallable interrupt handlers, it is often useful, and sometimes even
necessary, to be able to pass data between subsequent reinstalls of a handler. For
example, in
Section 6.4
we use reinstallable interrupt handlers to
implement a pseudorandom number generator in which it is crucial to be able
to pass and update a seed value between reinstalls of an interrupt handler. As
another example, consider wanting to react to only the first
n
interrupts of
a particular kind—here it is useful if we could pass and decrease a counter between
handler reinstalls.

In our original work
[
AP21
]
, such state-passing behaviour was achieved by
passing the relevant state values as arguments to the general-recursive functions that implemented
the reinstalling of interrupt handlers. However, with reinstallability of interrupt
handlers being now a primitive feature of
\lambda_{\text{\ae}}
, we want a similarly primitive approach to
managing state.

To this end, we extend the reinstallable interrupt handlers of last section with
state
:

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}promise}\;(\mathsf{op}\;x\,r\,\hbox to11.39pt{\vbox to6.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.69733pt\lower-3.00635pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.35 4.16 L -2.35 4.16 C -5.41 4.16 -7.88 1.68 -7.88 -1.37 L -7.88 1.37 C -7.88 -1.68 -5.41 -4.16 -2.35 -4.16 L 2.35 -4.16 C 5.41 -4.16 7.88 -1.68 7.88 1.37 L 7.88 -1.37 C 7.88 1.68 5.41 4.16 2.35 4.16 Z M -7.88 -4.16}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.84375pt}{-2.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\mapsto M)\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}@}_{\hbox to7pt{\vbox to6.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.5001pt\lower-3.24524pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.64653pt}{-2.39166pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\;\hbox to9.76pt{\vbox to8.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.88135pt\lower-4.27023pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.22 5.91 L -1.22 5.91 C -4.28 5.91 -6.75 3.43 -6.75 0.37 L -6.75 -0.37 C -6.75 -3.43 -4.28 -5.91 -1.22 -5.91 L 1.22 -5.91 C 4.28 -5.91 6.75 -3.43 6.75 -0.37 L 6.75 0.37 C 6.75 3.43 4.28 5.91 1.22 5.91 Z M -6.75 -5.91}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.02777pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}as}\;p\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;N

Here
S
denotes the type of state associated with a particular interrupt handler
(
S
can be an arbitrary value type),
s
is a variable bound in the interrupt handler code
M
,
giving it access to the handler’s state at the time of triggering, and
V
is the value of state
that is used at the next triggering of the interrupt handler. The state can be updated between
subsequent reinstalls of the interrupt handler by calling the reinstallation function
r
with the
new state value—
r
’s domain is now
S
instead of
\mathsf{1}
. This behaviour is summarised by the reduction rule

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}promise}\;(\mathsf{op}\;x\,r\,s\mapsto\!M)\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}@}_{S\!}\;W\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}as}\;p\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;N)\leadsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}let}\;p=M\big[V/x,R/r,\hbox to25.56pt{\vbox to11.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 12.78061pt\lower-5.85358pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\big]\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}in}\;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\downarrow}\,\mathsf{op}\,(V,N)

where

denotes a function that reinstalls the interrupt handler
with an updated state value:

Needing to know
S
for the function abstraction in

necessitates the type annotation on
this variant of interrupt handlers.
All other reduction rules remain unchanged, except that interrupt handlers now include additional variables, type annotations,
and values for states.

The typing rule for stateful reinstallable interrupt handlers is a straightforward
extension of the typing rule for reinstallable interrupt handlers we presented in the previous section:

\frac{({o^{\prime}},{\iota^{\prime}})\mathrel{\sqsubseteq_{O\times I}}\iota\,(\mathsf{op})\quad\Gamma,x\mathinner{:}A_{\mathsf{op}},r\mathinner{:}\hbox to13.42pt{\vbox to8.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.70773pt\lower-4.27023pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
